often called the Joker, is a subcomplex of the geometric fixed point of
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
if and only if
{j}_{24}=1
. Further, when
{j}_{24}=1
then in (
1.9
),
\epsilon
and
\delta
cannot both
equal
{\sf h}
. This can easily be derived from
Theorem 1.6
and
Theorem 1.19
.

Remark 1.22
.

In
[
6
]
, the authors construct
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[{\bf 0}]
as a split summand of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\wedge 3}
using a certain idempotent of
\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}[\Sigma_{3}]
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
be such that its cohomology as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
-module is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{R}}
, but has the additional relation

\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(a)=\rho\cdot c

(in the notation of
[
6
, Figure 3.6]
), as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module. If we replace
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{R}}
by a complex
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathbb{R}}
in
[
6
]
, we get
\mathrm{A}_{1}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
, where
\overline{\mathrm{v}}=(1,1,1,1,1,0,1)
(see the last diagram in
Figure 1.20
).

Remark 1.23
.

The classical spectrum
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is a type
2
spectrum and supports a
32
periodic
v_{2}
-self-map
[
4
]
. It remains to be seen if this
v_{2}
-self-map can be lifted to
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
for various
\overline{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathcal{V}
.

Recently in
[
5
]
, the authors introduced a new type
2
spectrum
\mathcal{Z}
which is notable for admitting a
v_{2}
-self-map of lowest possible periodicity, that is
1
. The low periodicity of the
v_{2}
-self-map makes the spectrum
\mathcal{Z}
suitable for the analysis of the telescope conjecture which, if true, would imply that the natural map from the telescope of
\mathcal{Z}
to the
\mathrm{K}(2)
-localization of
\mathcal{Z}
is a weak equivalence. While the telescope conjecture is true for finite spectra of type
1

[
17
,
18
,
21
]
, it is expected to be false for finite spectra of type
\geq 2
(see
[
16
]
). In fact, in
[
1
]
, the authors study the prime
2
, height
2
telescope conjecture
using the spectrum
\mathcal{Z}
and lay down several conjectures (see
[
1
]
*
\mathsection
9), whose validity would lead to a disproof of the telescope conjecture. In this paper, we also construct an
\mathbb{R}
-motivic analogue of
\mathcal{Z}
which is likely to shed light on some of these conjectures.

Theorem 1.24
.

There exists
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}
such that the underlying
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module structure of its cohomology is isomorphic to

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}})\cong_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)}\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)\otimes_{\Lambda(\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})}\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}

where
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}:=[\mathrm{Sq}^{4},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}]
.

In future work, we intend to study the properties of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}
extensively and hope to prove, among other things, the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.25
.

The spectrum
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}
is of type
(2,2)
and admits a
v_{(2,\mathrm{nil})}
-self-map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v:\Sigma^{6,3}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}}

of periodicity
1
.

Organization of the paper

In
Section 2
, we discuss the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization
Theorem 1.12
and derive various weak forms that are suitable for applications.
In
Section 3
, we construct the equivariant Steenrod operations using the equivariant extended power construction
and prove
Theorem 1.17
and
Theorem 1.19
, which
establish comparisons with the classical Steenrod operations. In
Section 4
, we apply the discussion in
Section 2
to obtain the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic topological realizations of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
and analyze the properties of their Betti realizations using results from
Section 3
. In
Section 5
, we construct the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic spectrum
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}
using a method of Smith. Finally, the short
Appendix A
lists the Adem relations in the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Steenrod algebra.

2.
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization theorem

The classical Toda realization theorem
[
25
]
(see also
[
5
]
*Theorem 3.1), is recast in the modern literature as a special case of Goerss-Hopkins obstruction theory
[
11
]
(when the chosen operad is trivial). This obstruction theory can be generalized to the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic setting
[
20
]
, and
Theorem 1.12
would then be a special case of such a generalization.

More recent work of
[
22
]
conceptualizes Goerss-Hopkins obstruction theory in the general setup of stable
\infty
-categories with
t
-structures. If we set
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathrm{A}=\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
, and let
\mathrm{K}
to be a finite
\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\mathbb{R}}
-comodule in
[
22
]
*Corollary 4.10, then we get a sequence of obstruction classes

(2.1)

\uptheta_{n}\in\Ext_{\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{-2,n+2,0}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{K})

for each
n\geq 0
, the vanishing of which guarantees the existence of an
\mathbb{S}_{\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
-module whose homology is isomorphic to
\mathrm{K}
as an
\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\mathbb{R}}
-comodule. Since the
t
-structure in
\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathbb{R}}
does not change the motivic weight, the obstruction classes in (
2.1
) lie in the Ext-groups of motivic weight
0
.

If
\mathrm{M}
is a finite
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module then
\mathrm{K}:=\hom_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is a finite
\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\mathbb{R}}
-comodule,

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{*,*,*}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{K})\cong\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{*,*,*}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{M}),

and therefore,
Theorem 1.12
follows. Alternatively, one can prove
Theorem 1.12
simply by emulating the classical proof (as exposed in
[
5
]
*
\mathsection 3
).

The purpose of this section is to prove various weaker forms of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization theorem (
Theorem 1.12
), which are perhaps more convenient for application purposes. Explicit calculation of
\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{*,*,*}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{M})
can often be difficult, and one can use a sequence of spectral sequences to approximate these ext groups. Each such approximation leads to a corresponding weaker form.

2.1.
Weak
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization – version (I)

Let
\mathrm{M}
be an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module whose underlying
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module is free and finitely generated. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}
denote its
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-basis and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
denote the collection of bidegrees in which there is an element in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}
. For any element
x\in\mathrm{M}^{s,w}
, we let
{\sf t}(x)=s+w
and define

\mathrm{M}_{\geq n}:=\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}:{\sf t}(b)\geq n\}

as the free sub
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module of
\mathrm{M}
generated by
\{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}:{\sf t}(b)\geq n\}
.

Note that the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure of
\mathrm{M}
is determined by the action of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
on the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}
and the Cartan formula. This, along with the fact that
{\sf t}(a)\geq 0
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
, implies that
\mathrm{M}_{\geq n}
are also a sub
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module of
\mathrm{M}
. Therefore, we get an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module filtration of
\mathrm{M}

\mathrm{M}=\mathrm{M}_{\geq k}\supset\mathrm{M}_{\geq k+1}\supset\dots\supset\mathrm{M}_{\geq k+l}={\bf 0}

such that we for each
i
there is a short exact sequences

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{M}_{\geq i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{M}_{\geq i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{\{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}:{\sf t}(b)=i\}}{\bigoplus}\Sigma^{|b|}\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-modules.

A short exact sequence of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-modules gives a long exact sequence in
\Ext
. By splicing the long exact sequences induced by (
2.2
), we get an “algebraic” Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence

(2.3)

\mathrm{E}_{2}^{s^{\prime},w^{\prime},s,f,w}:=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{M}}^{s^{\prime},w^{\prime}}\otimes\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{s,f,w}(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})\Rightarrow\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{s-s^{\prime},f,w-w^{\prime}}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{M})

and a corresponding weak version of
Theorem 1.12
, along with a uniqueness criterion, which is a weak form of
Theorem 1.14
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
denote an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module whose underlying
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module is free and finite. Suppose

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{s-2,f,w}(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})=0

for
f\geq 3
whenever
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Then there exists an
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}
such that

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathrm{X})\cong\mathrm{M}

as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module. Further, such a realization is unique if

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{s-1,f,w}(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})=0

for all
f\geq 2
and
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

2.2.
Weak
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization – version (II)

For any
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module
\mathrm{M}
which is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free, the quotient
\mathrm{M}/(\rho)
is an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
-module. In particular,

\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}/(\rho)\cong\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}

as a graded Hopf-algebra. Therefore, we have a spectral sequence

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{\rho}}\mathrm{E}_{2}^{s,f,w,i}:=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{s+i,f,w+i}(\mathrm{M}/(\rho),\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{s,f,w}(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})}

which is often called the
(algebraic)
\rho
-Bockstein spectral sequence
.
Thus we get the following version of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization and uniqueness theorem which is weaker than
Theorem 2.4
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
denote an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module whose underlying
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module is free and finite. Suppose

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{s-2+i,f,w+i}(\mathrm{M}/(\rho),\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})=0

for
f\geq 3
and all
i\geq 0
whenever
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
, then there exists an
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}
such that

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathrm{X})\cong\mathrm{M}

as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module. Further, such a realization is unique if

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{s-1+i,f,w+i}(\mathrm{M}/(\rho),\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})=0

for all
f\geq 2
,
i\geq 0
and
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

2.3.
Weak
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization – version (III)

Similarly to the classical case, the
\mathbb{C}
-motivic Steenrod algebra enjoys an increasing filtration called the May filtration, which is easier to express on its dual (see
[
10
]
).
On
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}
, the May filtration is induced by assigning the May weights

{\sf m}(\tau_{i-1})={\sf m}(\upxi_{i}^{2^{j}})=2i-1

and extending it multiplicatively. The associated graded is an exterior algebra

(2.7)

{\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}})}\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\upxi_{i,j}:i\geq 1,j\geq 0),

where
\upxi_{i,0}
represents
(\tau_{i-1})_{*}
and
(\upxi_{i,j+1})_{*}
represents
(\upxi_{i}^{2^{j}})_{*}
in the associated graded. When
\mathrm{M}=\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
in (
2.8
), then

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{*,*,*,*}\cong\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}[{\sf h}_{i,j}:i\geq 1,j\geq 0],

where
{\sf h}_{i,j}
represents the class
\upxi_{i,j}
. The
(s,f,w,{\sf m})
-degrees of these generators are given by

|{\sf h}_{i,j}|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cll}(2^{i}-2,1,2^{i-1}-1,i)&\text{if $j=0$, and, }\\
(2^{j}(2^{i}-1)-1,1,2^{j-1}(2^{i}-1),i)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

When
\mathrm{M}
is a cyclic
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module,
\mathrm{M}/(\rho)
is also cyclic as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
-module, thus the May filtration induces a filtration on
\mathrm{M}/(\rho)
. Thus, we get a corresponding May spectral sequence

(2.8)

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathrm{M}/(\rho)}^{s,f,w,{\sf m}}:=\Ext_{\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}})}^{s,f,w,m}(\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{M}/(\rho)),\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})\Rightarrow\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{s,f,w}(\mathrm{M}/(\rho),\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})

computing the input of the
\rho
-Bockstein spectral sequence (
2.5
). Thus we can formulate a version of
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization theorem which is even weaker than
Theorem 2.6
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathrm{M}
denote an cyclic
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module whose underlying
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module is free and finite. Suppose

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathrm{M}/(\rho)}^{s-2+i,f,w+i,*}=0.

for
f\geq 3
and all
i\geq 0
whenever
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Then there exists an
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}
such that

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathrm{X})\cong\mathrm{M}

as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module. Further, such a realization is unique if

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathrm{M}/(\rho)}^{s-1+i,f,w+i,*}=0

for
f\geq 2
,
i\geq 0
and
(s,w)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

3.
A comparison between
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant and classical squaring operations

For any
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
we can functorially assign two non-equivariant spaces – the underlying space
\Re(\mathrm{X})
, which is obtained by restricting the action of
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
to the trivial group, and the space of
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-fixed-points
{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
. For a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant spectrum
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
, restricting the action to the trivial subgroup results in a monoidal functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Re:\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sp}}

that identifies the underlying spectrum. However, there are two different notions of fixed-point spectrum – the categorial fixed-points and the geometric fixed-points.

The categorical fixed-points functor is a lax monodial functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}:\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sp},}

which is defined so that
\pi_{k}({\mathrm{E}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})\cong\pi_{k}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{E})
, but it does not interact well with infinite suspensions. The correction term is explained by the tom Dieck splitting
[
15
]
*Theorem V.11.1:

(3.1)

{(\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\mathrm{X})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})\vee\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{h}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}),

where
\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{h}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
is the homotopy orbit space. Let
\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}:=\mathrm{Cof}(\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}_{+}\to\mathbb{S})
. The geometric fixed-point functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi:\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sp},}

is a symmetric monoidal functor given by
\Phi(\mathrm{E}):={(\mathrm{E}\wedge\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
. When
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
,

(3.2)

\Phi(\Sigma^{\infty}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\mathrm{E})\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathrm{E}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}

is the first component in (
3.1
). For any
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
, there is a natural map of spectra

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota_{\mathrm{E}}:{\mathrm{E}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{E})}

induced by the map
\mathbb{S}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
.

The Eilenberg-Mac Lane spectrum
\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-ring (
[
15
]
*VII), i.e. a commutative monoid as a genuine
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-spectrum. The restriction
\Re(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the categorical fixed-points
{\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
and the geometric fixed-points
\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}[t]
are
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings.
It follows from the knowledge of
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}:=\pi_{\star}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Sigma^{n\upsigma}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\bigvee_{i=0}^{n}\Sigma^{i}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{colim}_{n}\ {(\Sigma^{n\upsigma}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}[t]}

is the inclusion of the first
(n+1)
components. The above map clearly splits. One can endow
{(\Sigma^{n\upsigma}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
with an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-structure isomorphic to the truncated polynomial algebra
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}[t]/(t^{n+1})
so that the splitting map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(n)}:\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Sigma^{n\upsigma}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}[t]/(t^{n+1})}

is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-map. The composition

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(0)}}

is the identity and exhibits
\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})
as an augmented
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-algebra.

For any
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}_{*}
, the restriction functor induces a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Re_{*}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{i,j}(\mathrm{X}_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{i}(\Re(\mathrm{X})_{+}).}

To compare the cohomology of
\mathrm{X}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
with the
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded cohomology of
\mathrm{X}
, we make use of the splitting (
3.3
)
to define the natural ring map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{i,j}(\mathrm{X}_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{i-j}(\mathrm{X}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+}),}

which sends
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{i,j}(\mathrm{X}_{+})
to the composite (as defined in
[
2
]
*(2.7))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}\mathrm{X}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{i-j}\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{i-j}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(0)}}

The purpose of this section is to compare the
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded squaring operations with the classical squaring operations along the maps
\Re_{*}
and
\hat{\Phi}_{*}
. We begin with a brief recollection of the construction of the classical and
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant squaring operations.

3.1.
Steenrod’s construction of squaring operations

The construction of the classical mod
2
Steenrod algebra, which is the algebra of stable cohomology operations for ordinary cohomology with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients, involves the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-structure
1
1

1

Technically, we only make use of the
\mathbb{H}_{\infty}
-ring structure that underlies the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-structure of
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
of
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
and the fact that the tautological line bundle
\upgamma
over
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-orientable.
We review here how the mod 2 Steenrod operations are derived from that structure. A similar discussion can be found in
[
7
]
*Section VIII.2.

Notation 3.4
.

For any space or spectrum
\mathrm{X}
and
n\geq 1
, we let

\mathrm{D}_{n}(\mathrm{X}):=(\mathrm{E}\Sigma_{n})_{+}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}(\mathrm{X}^{\wedge n}),

where
\Sigma_{n}
acts by permuting the factors of
\mathrm{X}^{\wedge n}
. By convention,
\mathrm{D}_{0}(\mathrm{X})=\mathbb{S}
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring structure on a spectrum
\mathrm{R}
is a collection of maps of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{D}_{n}(\mathrm{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}

for each
n\geq 0
, which satisfy the usual coherence conditions (see
[
19
]
). By assumption,
\Theta_{0}^{\mathrm{R}}
is the unit map of
\mathrm{R}
and
\Theta_{1}^{\mathrm{R}}
is the identity map.

The
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-orientibility of
\upgamma
implies the existence of an
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Thom class

(3.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf u}_{n}:\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus n})\simeq\mathbb{RP}_{n}^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}

for each
n\geq 0
. These are compatible as
n
varies, in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

(3.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus(m+n)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus m})\wedge\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf u}_{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf u}_{m}\wedge{\sf u}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}

For any spectra
\mathrm{E}
and
\mathrm{F}
, there is a natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\updelta_{n}:\mathrm{D}_{n}(\mathrm{E}\wedge\mathrm{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{n}(\mathrm{E})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{n}(\mathrm{F})}

induced by the diagonal on
\mathrm{E}\Sigma_{n}
and the isomorphism
(\mathrm{E}\wedge\mathrm{F})^{\wedge n}\cong\mathrm{E}^{\wedge n}\wedge\mathrm{F}^{\wedge n}
.
Thus, we may define the map
\uptau_{n}
as the composition

(3.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{S}^{n})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{RP}_{n}^{\infty}\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\updelta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uptau_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}{\sf u}_{n}\wedge\Theta_{2}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}

Definition 3.8
.

The power operation is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}\colon\mathrm{H}^{n}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{2n}(\mathrm{D}_{2}(-)),}

which takes a class
u\in\mathrm{H}^{n}(\mathrm{E})
to the composite class

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{2}(u)\colon\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\uptau}_{n}}

for any
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}
.

From (
3.6
), we deduce the commutativity of the diagram

(3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\Sigma^{m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\Sigma^{2m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n+m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n+2m}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uptau_{n}\wedge\uptau_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uptau_{n+m}}

As a result, we have

\updelta_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(u)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{2}(v))=\mathcal{P}_{2}(u\otimes v)

which leads to the Cartan formula for the Steenrod algebra.

If
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}
is given the trivial
\Sigma_{2}
-action and
\mathrm{X}\wedge\mathrm{X}
the permutation action, the diagonal map
\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}\wedge\mathrm{X}
is
\Sigma_{2}
-equivariant. Consequently, we have an induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}:(\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge\mathrm{X}\simeq(\mathrm{E}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge_{\Sigma_{2}}\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{X}).}

Since
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[{\sf t}]
, we may write (using the Kunneth isomorphism)

(3.10)

\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(u))=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf t}^{n-i}\otimes\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(u),

which defines the natural transformations
\mathrm{Sq}^{i}\colon\mathrm{H}^{n}(-)\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n+i}(-)
.

Remark 3.11
.

The squaring operation
\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(u)
for any class
u\in\mathrm{H}^{n}(\mathrm{X})
is determined by
\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\iota_{n})
, where
\iota_{n}\in\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{F}_{2},n))
is the fundamental class, because of the universal property of
\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{F}_{2},n)
. A priori,
\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(u)
depends on the cohomological degree of
u
. However, this dependence is eradicated by the fact that the squaring operations are stable, i.e. for any
u\in\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{X})

\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\sigma_{*}(u))=\sigma_{*}(\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(u)),

where
\sigma_{*}:\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{X})\cong\mathrm{H}^{\ast+1}(\Sigma\mathrm{X})
is the suspension isomorphism. The
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-orientibility of
\upgamma
implies
\mathrm{Sq}^{0}(\iota)=\iota
for the generator
\iota\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathrm{S}^{1})
, which, along with Cartan formula, implies stability.

3.2.
The
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant squaring operations

The construction of the classical squaring operations can be adapted to construct squaring operations on the
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded cohomology of a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-space.

Remark 3.12
.

Our ideas are closely related to the construction of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic squaring operations due to Voevodsky
[
26
]
. Certain parts, such as the construction of the power operation
Definition 3.19
, though different, can be compared to
[
27
,
28
]
, where the author studies
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant power operations on the homology of spaces.

Notation 3.13
.

For any group
\mathrm{G}
and a family of subgroups
\mathcal{F}
closed under subconjugacy, there exists a space
\mathrm{E}\mathcal{F}

determined up to a
\mathrm{G}
-weak equivalence by its universal property

\mathrm{E}\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{H}}\simeq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\ast&\text{if $\mathrm{H}\in\mathcal{F},$}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

When
\mathrm{G}={\mathrm{C}_{2}}\times\Sigma_{n}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\{\mathrm{H}\subset\mathrm{G}:\mathrm{H}\cap\Sigma_{n}=\mathbbm{1}\}
, we denote
\mathrm{E}\mathcal{F}_{n}
by
\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{n}
.
Note that there is a natural
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant map

\mathrm{E}\Sigma_{n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\Sigma_{n}
.

Notation 3.14
.

For a based
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-space or a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-spectrum
\mathrm{X}
, we let

\mathrm{D}_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X}):=(\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{n})_{+}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}(\mathrm{X}^{\wedge n})

the
n
-th equivariant extended power construction on
\mathrm{X}
. There is a natural
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\updelta_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}:\mathrm{D}_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X}\wedge\mathrm{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{Y})}

induced by the diagonal map of
\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{n}
for any pair
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{Y}
of
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
space or spectra.

For a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
, the inclusions
\mathrm{X}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{E}\Sigma_{n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\Sigma_{n}
together induce a natural map

(3.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{X}}:\mathrm{D}_{2}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}

which is usually not an equivalence.

Example 3.16
.

When
\mathrm{X}\simeq\mathrm{S}^{0}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}:(\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2}\wedge\mathrm{S}^{0}_{+}}

is the inclusion of a summand.

Likewise, when
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
, the map
\mathrm{E}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{E}
induces a natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{E}}:\mathrm{D}_{2}({\mathrm{E}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{E})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}.}

Using the fact that
\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-spectrum
we define a map
\uplambda^{\Phi}_{\mathrm{E}}
as the composition

(3.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Phi(\mathrm{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{E}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{D}_{2}(\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\wedge\mathrm{E}))^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{D}_{2}(\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{E}))}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{E}))}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\cong\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{E}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda^{\Phi}_{\mathrm{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\wedge\mathrm{E}}}

By definition, an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-ring structure on a spectrum
\mathrm{R}
consists of a system of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{D}_{n}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}

for each
n\geq 0
, which satisfy certain compatibility criteria
[
15
]
*§VII.2.
The categorical fixed-point spectrum
{\mathrm{R}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
as well as the geometric-fixed point spectrum
\Phi(\mathrm{R})
of an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-ring spectrum
\mathrm{R}
are
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring spectra with structure maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{n}^{{\mathrm{R}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}:\mathrm{D}_{2}({\mathrm{R}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{R})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{R}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{R}})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{n}^{\Phi(\mathrm{R})}:\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Phi(\mathrm{R}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{R}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{R}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}^{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\Theta_{n}^{\mathrm{R}})}

respectively. Further, the natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota_{\mathrm{R}}:{\mathrm{R}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{R})}

is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring map.

Let
\upomega
denote the sign representation of
\Sigma_{2}
. The equivariant Eilenberg-Mac Lane spectrum
\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
does not distinguish between the
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\upepsilon}:\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}\times_{\Sigma_{2}}(\uprho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\upgamma}:\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}\times_{\Sigma_{2}}(\uprho\otimes\upomega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2},}

i.e. there exists a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant Thom isomorphism

\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma})\wedge\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\simeq\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upepsilon})\wedge\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\simeq\Sigma^{\uprho}(\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}.

The above Thom isomorphism results in an
\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
-Thom class

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\sf u}}_{n}:\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n\rho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}

for each
n\geq 0
, and these Thom classes can be used to define the
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant power operations. Since

\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{S}^{n\uprho})\simeq\mathrm{Th}(n\uprho\oplus n(\uprho\otimes\upomega))\simeq\Sigma^{n\uprho}\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n}),

we define the map
\underline{\uptau}_{n}
as the composition

(3.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n\rho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n\rho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}(\mathrm{S}^{n\rho})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n\uprho}\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n\rho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\uptau}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}\underline{{\sf u}}_{n}\wedge\Theta_{2}^{\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}

Definition 3.19
.

The equivariant power operation is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{2n\uprho}(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(-)),}

which takes a class
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{E})
to the composite class

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u):\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\Sigma^{n\uprho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n\uprho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\uptau}_{n}}

for any
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
.

When
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
is given the trivial
\Sigma_{2}
-action and
\mathrm{X}\wedge\mathrm{X}
is given the permutation action, the diagonal map
\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{X}\wedge\mathrm{X}
is a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\times\Sigma_{2}
-equivariant map. Consequently, we have a
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\!{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{\mathrm{X}}:(\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge\mathrm{X}\simeq(\mathrm{E}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge_{\Sigma_{2}}\mathrm{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X}).}

By
[
14
]
*Lemma 6.27 (also see
[
27
]
*Proposition 3.2),

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+})\cong\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}[{\sf y},{\sf x}]/({\sf y}^{2}=a_{\upsigma}{\sf y}+u_{\upsigma}{\sf x}),

where
|{\sf y}|=(1,1)
and
|{\sf x}|=(2,1)
.
Since
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-free, we also have a Kunneth isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge\mathrm{X})\cong\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+})\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{X}).

Thus, for any
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
, we may write
(\Delta^{\!{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{\mathrm{X}})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u))
using the formula

(3.20)

(\Delta^{\!{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{\mathrm{X}})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u))=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf x}^{n-i}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i}(u)+\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf y}{\sf x}^{n-i-1}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i+1}(u),

which defines the equivariant squaring operations
\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. These can be extended to operations on the entire
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded cohomology ring as in
[
26
]
*Prop 2.6).

Remark 3.21
.

Just like the classical case, one can easily deduce that the
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded squaring operations defined this way are natural, stable and obey the Cartan formula. In fact, Voevodsky
[
26
]
uses a similar approach to establish these properties for the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Steenrod algebra, which can be emulated in the
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant case using the Betti realization functor.

3.3.
Comparison theorems

Since the restriction functor is monoidal, it induces a ring map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Re_{*}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{X}_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{\ast}(\Re(\mathrm{X})_{+})}

for any
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{*}
.

Example 3.22
.

When
\mathrm{X}=\ast
,
the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Re_{*}:\pi_{\star}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}}

sends
u_{\upsigma}\mapsto 1
,
a_{\upsigma}\mapsto 0
, and
\Theta\mapsto 0
. This follows from the fact that the cofiber sequence
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}_{+}\longrightarrow S^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{\upsigma}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{\upsigma}
shows that the kernel of
\Re_{*}
consists of precisely the
a_{\upsigma}
-divisible elements.

Proposition 3.23
.

For any
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
and a class
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})

\Re_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u))=\mathcal{P}_{2n}(\Re_{*}(u)).

Proof.

Since,
\Re(\overline{\upgamma})=2\upgamma
, it follows that
\Re_{*}(\underline{{\sf u}}_{n})={\sf u}_{2n}
. This, along with the fact that
\Re(\Theta_{2}^{\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}})=\Theta_{2}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
shows
\Re(\underline{\uptau}_{n})=\uptau_{2n}
, and the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 1.17
.

Let
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
and
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
. Since
\Re(\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})\simeq\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2}
,
\Re(\Delta^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})=\Delta
,

\Re_{*}({\sf y})={\sf t}
and
\Re_{*}({\sf x})={\sf t}^{2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Re(\mathrm{X})}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}(\Re_{*}(u)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=-n}^{n}{\sf t}^{n-i}\otimes\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\Re_{*}(u))

must equal

\displaystyle{\Re_{*}((\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u)))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re_{*}(\sum_{i=-n}^{n}{\sf x}^{n-i}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i}(u)+\sum_{i=-n}^{n}{\sf y}{\sf x}^{n-i-1}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i+1}(u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=-n}^{n}{\sf t}^{2n-2i}\otimes\Re_{*}(\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i}(u))

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+\sum_{i=-n}^{n}{\sf t}^{2n-2i-1}\otimes\Re_{*}(\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i+1}(u)).

Thus, the result is true for cohomology classes
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
for any space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
.

Since the squaring operations are stable, the result extends to arbitrary
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded cohomology classes. Moreover, since
\Re
commutes with suspensions, in the sense that

\Re\circ{\Sigma_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}^{\infty}}\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}\circ\Re
,
and any
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
is equivalent to
\Sigma^{-n}\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\mathrm{X}
for some
n
and
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
, we conclude the same for any
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{E})
.
∎

Now we draw our attention towards comparing the action of the
{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-equivariant Steenrod algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
on
\mathrm{H}^{\star}(\mathrm{X}_{+})
to the action of the classical Steenrod algebra
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathrm{H}^{*}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+})
, where
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
. Note that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{X}_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+})}

is a ring map.

Example 3.24
.

When
\mathrm{X}=\ast
, the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}\colon\pi_{\star}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[u_{\upsigma},a_{\upsigma}]\oplus\Theta\{u_{\upsigma}^{-i}a_{\upsigma}^{-j}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}}

sends
a_{\upsigma}\mapsto 1
,
u_{\upsigma}\mapsto 0
, and
\Theta\mapsto 0
. This is essentially because smashing with

\widetilde{\mathrm{E}{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\simeq\mathrm{colim}\{\mathrm{S}^{0}\overset{a_{\upsigma}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{S}^{\upsigma}\overset{a_{\upsigma}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{S}^{2\upsigma}\longrightarrow\dots\}

amounts to inverting
a_{\upsigma}
and the projection
\uppi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(0)}
kills
u_{\upsigma}
.

Remark 3.25
.

One can deduce from
Example 3.16
that in cohomology, the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}:\mathrm{H}^{*}({\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[{\sf t}][\iota]/(\iota^{2}-\iota)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}((\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[{\sf t}],}

is the quotient map sending
\iota\mapsto 0
.

Example 3.26
.

The map
\hat{\Phi}_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+})\to\mathrm{H}^{\ast}({\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+})
sends

{\sf x}\mapsto{\sf t}
and
{\sf y}\to\iota
,
a_{\upsigma}\mapsto 1
and
u_{\upsigma}\mapsto 0
.

Lemma 3.27
.

The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{\ast}({\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus n}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{\uprho\otimes\upomega}}}

sends
\underline{{\sf u}}_{n}\mapsto{\sf u}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\upzeta_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}:(\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\to\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n})
denote the zero-section. Under the zero section map the Thom class is mapped to the Euler class, and therefore
\upzeta_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}^{*}(\underline{{\sf u}}_{n})={\sf x}^{n}
. Likewise, the zero-section for the nonequivariant bundle
\upzeta:(\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\to\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus n})
sends
{\sf u}_{n}\mapsto{\sf t}^{n}.
By naturality of
\hat{\Phi}_{*}
and
\uplambda
, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Th}(\overline{\upgamma}^{\oplus n})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Th}(\upgamma^{\oplus n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!((\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}({\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{*}((\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upzeta_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{\uprho\otimes\upomega}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\upzeta}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upzeta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}}

which along with
Remark 3.25
and injectivity of
\upzeta^{*}
implies the result.
∎

Corollary 3.28
.

For any space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
and a class
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
,

(3.29)

\mathcal{P}_{n}(\hat{\Phi}_{*}(u))=\uplambda^{*}_{\mathrm{X}}(\hat{\Phi}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n\uprho}(u))).

Proof.

It is enough to show that in the following diagram commutes as the blue path and the red path indicates the left-hand side and the right-hand side of (
3.29
) respectively.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Phi(\Sigma^{n\uprho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\Sigma^{n\uprho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{S}^{n})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\mathrm{S}^{n})\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}(\mathrm{S}^{n\uprho}))\wedge\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}\wedge\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\Sigma^{2n\uprho}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\wedge\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\Phi(\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Phi(u))}
(A)
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(u))}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\updelta_{2}}
(B)
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}(\Sigma^{n}\uppi^{(0)}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}})}
(C)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda^{\Phi}_{\Sigma^{n\uprho}\wedge\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\updelta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\updelta_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf u}_{n}\wedge\Theta_{2}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
(E)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}\wedge\mathrm{D}_{2}(\uppi^{(0)}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf u}_{n}\wedge\Theta_{2}^{\Phi\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
(F)
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\sf u}}_{n}\wedge\Theta_{2}^{\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\upmu_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
(G)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}\wedge\uppi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\uppi^{(0)}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\wedge\mathbbm{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\Phi(\upmu_{\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2n}\uppi^{(0)}_{\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}}

The squares (A), (B) and (C) commute naturally, the squares (E) and (G) commute because
\pi_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}^{(0)}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring map, and (F) commutes because of
Lemma 3.27
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 1.19
.

For any space
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
and a class
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{B}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}_{2}({\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{B}_{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}\Sigma_{2})_{+}\wedge{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{D}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}(\mathrm{X})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}\wedge\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uplambda_{\mathrm{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf t}^{n-i}\otimes\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\Phi_{*}(u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{n}(\Phi_{*}(u)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*}(\uplambda^{*}_{\mathrm{X}}(\Phi_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2n,n}(u))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*})(({(\Delta_{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}})^{*}(\Phi_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2n,n}(u))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*})\Phi_{*}((\Delta^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}_{\mathrm{X}})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{2n,n}(u)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*})\Phi_{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf x}^{n-i}\otimes\mathrm{Sq}^{2i,i}(u)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt\left.+\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf y}{\sf x}^{n-i-1}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i+1}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\uplambda_{\mathrm{S}^{0}}^{*}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{X}}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}^{*})\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf t}^{n-i}\otimes\Phi_{*}(\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i}(u))\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt\left.+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\iota{\sf t}^{n-i-1}\otimes\Phi_{*}(\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i+1}(u))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\sf t}^{n-i}\otimes\Phi_{*}(\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{2i}(u)),

and hence, the result is true for all
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{n\uprho}(\mathrm{X})
for any
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
.

Since the squaring operations are stable, the result extends to arbitrary
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded cohomology classes. Moreover, since
the geometric fixed point functor
\Phi
commutes with suspensions (
3.2
),
and any
\mathrm{E}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
is equivalent to
\Sigma^{-n}\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}\mathrm{X}
for some
n
and
\mathrm{X}\in\mathbf{Top}_{*}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
, we conclude the same for any
u\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}(\mathrm{E})
.
∎

4.
Topological realization of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)

We begin by proving
Theorem 1.6
, which identifies all possible
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structures on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
up to isomorphism.

Figure 4.1.
This figure displays the free
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
, with
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}
-multiplications drawn in only on the
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module generators.

Proof of
Theorem 1.6
.

Note that the Cartan formula of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
and finiteness of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
imply that the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
is determined once the action of
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{8}
are specified on its
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-generators.
The following are possible
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{8}
-actions on the
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module generators. As can be seen in
Figure 4.1
, there is no room for other possible actions.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(x_{0,0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{03}(\rho\cdot y_{3,1})+\beta_{04}(\tau\cdot y_{4,1})+\alpha_{03}(\rho\cdot x_{3,1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(x_{1,0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{14}(\rho\cdot y_{4,1})+\beta_{15}(y_{5,2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(x_{2,1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{j}_{24}(\rho^{2}\cdot y_{4,1})+\beta_{25}(\rho\cdot y_{5,2})+\beta_{26}(\tau\cdot y_{6,2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(x_{3,1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{36}(\rho\cdot y_{6,2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{4}(y_{3,1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma_{36}(\rho\cdot y_{6,2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{8}(x_{0,0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{06}(\rho^{2}\cdot y_{6,2})

The Adem relation
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{3}=\mathrm{Sq}^{5}+\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}+\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
(see
Proposition A.1
), when applied to
x_{0,0}
and
x_{2,1}
, yields
\beta_{15}=1
,
\beta_{03}+\beta_{04}+\beta_{14}=1
and
\beta_{25}+\beta_{26}=\beta_{36}
. The equation

{j}_{24}=\beta_{03}\gamma_{36}+\alpha_{03}\beta_{36},

is forced by the Adem relation
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}=\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}+\tau\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
when
applied to
x_{0,0}
. This exhausts all constraints imposed by Adem relations in these dimensions.
∎

In
Theorem 1.6
, there are exactly seven free variables taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, and therefore, there are exactly 128 different
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
. Thus, in order to complete the proof of
Theorem 1.3
, we realize these
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-modules as spectra using
Theorem 2.9
, which is a weak form of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda realization theorem.

Proof of
Theorem 1.3
.

Firstly, note that
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)
is a cyclic
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module for all
\overline{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathcal{V}
, therefore
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1):=\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)/(\rho)
admits a May filtration. Secondly, note that

\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1))\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\upxi_{1,0},\upxi_{1,1},\upxi_{2,0})

as an
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}})
-module (see (
2.7
) for notation). Consequently,

(4.2)

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)}^{*,*,*,*}\cong{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{*,*,*,*}/({\sf h}_{1,0},{\sf h}_{1,1},{\sf h}_{2,0})\cong\frac{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}[{\sf h}_{i,j}:i\geq 1,j\geq 0]}{({\sf h}_{1,0},{\sf h}_{1,1},{\sf h}_{2,0})}.

In the notation of
Subsection 2.3

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)}=\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,1),(3,1),(4,1),(5,2),(6,2)\}

By directly inspecting the
(s,f,w)
-degree of
{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathcal{A}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}^{\mathbb{C}}(1)}^{*,*,*,*}
,
we see that the condition necessary for existence in
Theorem 2.9
is satisfied. Hence, the result.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The vanishing region of
{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathcal{A}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}^{\mathbb{C}}(1)}^{*,*,*,*}
does not preclude the possibility of having a nonzero element in
\Ext_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1,2,0}(\mathrm{M},\mathrm{M})
. We suspect (even after running the differentials in (
2.3
) and (
2.5
)), that the above group is nonzero for a given
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
, and that there are, up to homotopy, multiple realizations as
\mathbb{R}
-motivic spectra.

Our next goal is to prove
Theorem 1.8
. We begin with the following observation.

Lemma 4.4
.

The
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-modules
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
and
\mathcal{B}_{{\sf h}}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
are uniquely realizable as objects in
\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Both
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
and
\mathcal{B}_{{\sf h}}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
are cyclic
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free finite
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module and

\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)/(\rho)\cong\mathcal{B}_{{\sf h}}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)/(\rho)

as
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
-module. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{C}}(1):=\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)/(\rho)
.
It is easy to see that
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{C}}(1))
is isomorphic to
\Lambda_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\upxi_{1,0},\upxi_{1,1})

as an
\mathrm{gr}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}})
-module, and therefore

(4.5)

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{C}}(1)}^{*,*,*,*}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[\tau][h_{i,j}:i\geq 1,j\geq 0]/({\sf h}_{1,0},{\sf h}_{1,1}).

Using this, along with the fact that

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{C}}(1)}=\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,1),(3,1),\}

shows that the condition necessary for existence as well as uniqueness in
Theorem 2.9
is satisfied. Hence, the result.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 1.8
.

Consider the injective
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module map
\Sigma^{3,1}\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)\to\mathrm{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}
sending the
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
generator in degree
(3,1)
to
x_{3,1}+y_{3,1}
. It follows from
Theorem 1.6
that the quotient is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
. Thus, we have the exact sequence (
1.9
).

The topological realization of (
1.9
), i.e. the cofiber sequence (
1.10
), would follow immediately from
Lemma 4.4
once we show that any one of the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module maps in (
1.9
) can be realized as a map in
\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus, it is enough to show that the nonzero class in the
\mathrm{E}_{2}
-page represented by the projection map
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
in degree
(0,0,0)
of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Adams spectral sequence

(4.6)

\mathrm{E}_{2}^{s,f,w}:=\Ext^{s,f,w}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1),\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(1))\Rightarrow\left[\mathcal{Y}_{(\delta,1)}^{\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]\right]_{s,w}

is a nonzero permanent cycle.

Using (
4.2
), the
\rho
-Bockstein spectral sequence (
2.5
) for
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
and the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence

\mathcal{B}^{s^{\prime},w^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)}\otimes\Ext^{s,f,w}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{1}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}],\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})\Rightarrow\Ext^{s+s^{\prime},f,w+w^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{1}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}],\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)),

one can easily check
\mathrm{E}_{2}^{-1,f,0}=0
for all
f\geq 2
, and thus any nonzero element in degree
(0,0,0)
of the
\mathrm{E}_{2}
-page in (
4.6
) is, in fact, a nonzero permanent cycle.
∎

Our next goal is to analyze the underlying spectrum and geometric fixed-points spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
, the Betti realization of
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
.

4.1.
The Betti realization of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{1}

Under the Betti-realization map

(4.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upbeta_{*}:\pi_{\ast,\ast}\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[\rho,\tau]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\star}\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}

\rho\mapsto a_{\upsigma}
and
\tau\mapsto u_{\upsigma}
. Since the functor
\upbeta
is symmetric monoidal and
\upbeta(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{F}_{2})=\mathrm{H}\underline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, the
i
-th
\mathbb{R}
-motivic squaring operations maps to the
i
-th
\mathrm{RO}({\mathrm{C}_{2}})
-graded squaring operations under the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upbeta_{*}:\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}.}

Hence,
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}])
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-free (as
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}])
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free) and its
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-module structure is essentially given by
Theorem 1.6
(after replacing
\mathrm{Sq}^{i}
with
\underline{\mathrm{Sq}}^{i}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-basis elements by its image under
\upbeta_{*}
).

Remark 4.8
.

The map
\upbeta_{*}
of (
4.7
) is only an injection with cokernel the summand
\Theta\{u_{\upsigma}^{-i}a_{\upsigma}^{-j}:i,j\geq 0\}
of
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
. In general, for an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(1)
-module
\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{R}}
, the number of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}
-module structures on
\upbeta(\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{R}})
can be strictly larger than the number of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structures on
\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{R}}
. But this is not the case when
\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)
simply for degree reasons, therefore
Corollary 1.15
holds.

As discussed in
Example 3.22
,
the restriction map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Re_{*}:\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}}

sends
a_{\upsigma}\mapsto 0
,
u_{\upsigma}\mapsto 1
, and
\Theta\mapsto 0
.
Thus, when
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{E})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-free,
\Re_{*}
is simply “setting
a_{\upsigma}=0
,
u_{\upsigma}=1
, and
\Theta=0
”.
This observation, along with
Theorem 1.17
, allows us to completely deduce the
\mathcal{A}
-module structure of
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Re(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\overline{\mathrm{v}}}(1)))
from
Theorem 1.6
.
Together with the fact that the
\mathcal{A}
-module structures on
\mathcal{A}(1)
are uniquely-realized, our observations yield
the following theorem, where the notation
\mathrm{A}_{1}[i,j]
is adopted from
[
4
]
.

Theorem 4.9
.

For
\overline{\mathrm{v}}=(\alpha_{03},\beta_{03},\beta_{14},\beta_{06},\beta_{25},\beta_{26},\gamma_{36})\in\mathcal{V}
(as in
Theorem 1.6
),

\Re(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}])\simeq\mathrm{A}_{1}[1+\beta_{03}+\beta_{14},\beta_{26}].

Now we shift our attention towards understand the geometric fixed-points of
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}]
.
As discussed in
Example 3.24
, the modified geometric fixed-points functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Phi}_{*}\colon\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}}

sends
a_{\upsigma}\mapsto 1
,
u_{\upsigma}\mapsto 0
, and
\Theta\mapsto 0
. Thus, when
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathrm{E})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{{\mathrm{C}_{2}}}
-free,
\hat{\Phi}_{*}
is simply “setting
a_{\upsigma}=1
,
u_{\upsigma}=0
, and
\Theta=0
”. This, along with
Theorem 1.6
and
Theorem 1.19
, gives the following.

Notation 4.10
.

Because
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{C}_{2}}^{\star}\!(\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathrm{C}_{2}}[\overline{\mathrm{v}}])

. We let

,

,
s_{2}:=\hat{\Phi}_{*}(y_{3,1})

\text{$t_{2}:=\hat{\Phi}_{*}(x_{3,1})$, $t_{3a}:=\hat{\Phi}_{*}(y_{5,2})$, $t_{3b}:=\hat{\Phi}_{*}(y_{4,1})$, $t_{4}:=\hat{\Phi}_{*}(y_{6,2})$}.

Note that
|s_{i(-)}|=|t_{i(-)}|=i
.

.

The

5.
An
\mathbb{R}
-motivic analogue of the spectrum
\mathcal{Z}

The type
2
spectrum
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}
, introduced in
[
5
]
, is defined by the property that its cohomology as an
\mathcal{A}(2)
-module is

\mathcal{B}(2):=\mathcal{A}(2)\otimes_{\Lambda(\mathrm{Q}_{2})}\mathbb{F}_{2},

where
\mathrm{Q}_{2}=[\mathrm{Sq}^{4},\mathrm{Q}_{1}]
is dual to the Milnor generator
\upxi_{3}
of the dual Steenrod algebra. They first show that an
\mathcal{A}
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}(2)
satisfying the criteria in
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.7 leads to an
\mathcal{A}
-module structure on
\mathcal{B}(2)
. In
[
5
]
, the authors show that among the 1600 possible
\mathcal{A}
-module structures on
\mathcal{A}(2)

[
24
]
, there are some
\mathcal{A}
-modules that satisfy
[
5
]
*Lemma 2.7. Then they use the classical Toda realization theorem to show that any
\mathcal{A}
-module whose underlying
\mathcal{A}(2)
-module structure is
\mathcal{B}(2)
can be realized as a
2
-local finite spectrum, which they call
\mathcal{Z}
.

We construct
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
by emulating the construction of the classical
\mathcal{Z}
(as in
[
5
]
) in the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic context. Since there is no a priori
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
,
we produce one in the following subsection. In fact, we construct an
\mathbb{R}
-motivic spectrum whose cohomology is the desired
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module.

5.1.
A topological realization of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
denote the sub-
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Steenrod algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
generated by
\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}
, and
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
. We will use a method of Smith (exposed in
[
23
]
*Appendix C) to construct an
\mathbb{R}
-motivic spectrum
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
such that its cohomology as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module is free on one generator.

Let
{\sf h},\eta_{1,1}
and
\nu_{3,2}
denote the first three
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Hopf-elements.

Lemma 5.1
.

The
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda-bracket
\langle{\sf h},\eta_{1,1},\nu_{3,2}\rangle
contains
0
.

Proof.

In this argument, it will be convenient to refer to the “coweight”, by which we mean the difference
s-w
, as in
[
12
]
.

Since
{\sf h}
and
\eta_{1,1}
have coweight
0
while
\nu_{3,2}
has coweight
1
, it follows that the bracket
\langle{\sf h},\eta_{1,1},\nu_{3,2}\rangle
is comprised of elements in stem
5
with coweight
2
. The only element in stem 5 with coweight 1 is
\rho\cdot\nu_{3,2}^{2}

[
3
]
. Since this element is a
\nu_{3,2}
multiple, it lies in the indeterminacy, which means that the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Toda-bracket does contain zero.
∎

Lemma 5.1
implies that we can construct a
4
-cell complex
\mathcal{K}
whose cohomology as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module has the structure described in
Corollary 5.2
and displayed in
Figure 5.3
.

Corollary 5.2
.

There exists
\mathcal{K}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{K})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free on four generators
x_{0},x_{1},x_{3}
and
x_{7}
, such that
\mathrm{Sq}^{i+1}(x_{i})=x_{2i+1}
for
i\in\{0,1,3\}
.

Figure 5.3.
We depict the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-structure of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{K})
by marking the
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}
-action by black straight lines, the
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}
-action by blue curved lines, and the
\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
-action by red lines between the
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-generators.

Let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}[\Sigma_{6}]
denote the idempotent corresponding to the Young tableaux

6
4
1
5
3
2

which is constructed as follows. Let
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Row}}\subset\Sigma_{6}
denote the subgroup comprised of permutations that preserve each row. Likewise, let
\Sigma_{\mathrm{Col}}
denote the subgroup comprised of column-preserving permutations.
Let

(5.4)

{\sf R}=\sum_{r\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{Row}}}r\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\sf C}=\sum\limits_{c\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{Col}}}(-1)^{\mathrm{sign}(c)}c

and define

e=\frac{1}{\mu}{\sf R}\cdot{\sf C},

where
\mu
is an odd integer defined in
[
23
, Theorem C.1.3]
. We let
\overline{e}
denote the resulting idempotent in
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\Sigma_{6}]
.

Proposition 5.5
.

The idempotent
\overline{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}[\Sigma_{6}]
has the property that
\overline{e}(\mathrm{V}^{\otimes 6})=0
if
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathrm{V}<3
and

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\overline{e}(\mathrm{V}^{\otimes 6})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}8&\text{ if $\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathrm{V}=3$}\\
64&\text{ if $\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathrm{V}=4$}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Let
\overline{{\sf R}}
and
\overline{{\sf C}}
denote the images of
{\sf R}
and
{\sf C}
in
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\Sigma_{6}]
, respectively. Then
\overline{e}=\overline{{\sf R}}\cdot\overline{{\sf C}}
. It is straightforward that
\overline{{\sf C}}
vanishes on
\mathrm{V}^{\otimes 6}
if
\dim\mathrm{V}\leq 2
.

Now suppose that
\mathrm{V}
has basis
\{a,b,c\}

Finally, suppose that
\dim\mathrm{V}=4
with basis
\{a,b,c,d\}
. For any subspace
\mathrm{W}\subset\mathrm{V}
spanned by three of these basis elements, the space
\overline{e}(\mathrm{W}^{\otimes 6})
has dimension 8, as we have just seen. There are
4
choices of
\mathrm{W}
, which together yield a 32-dimensional subspace of
\mathrm{V}^{\otimes 6}
. Now consider Young tableaux in which all four basis elements appear and only one is repeated. In the case that
d

}\hskip-8.61108pt\right).

As there are
\binom{4}{2}=6
such choices, this contributes another subspace of dimension
4\cdot 6=24
.
∎

We define

\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2}:=\Sigma^{-5,-1}e(\mathcal{K}^{\wedge 6})=\Sigma^{-5,-1}(\mathrm{hocolim}\ \{\mathcal{K}^{\wedge 6}\overset{e}{\to}\mathcal{K}^{\wedge 6}\overset{e}{\to}\dots\}),

which is a split summand of
\Sigma^{-5,-1}\mathcal{K}^{\wedge 6}
as
e
is an idempotent. We shift the grading by
(-5,-1)
to make sure that the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module generator of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2})
is in
(0,0)
(see
Remark 5.12
).

Theorem 5.6
.

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})\cong\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module.

Proof.

By
[
6
, Corollary 2.7]
,
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is a free
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module
if and only if
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is free as an
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module and
the Margolis homology
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbb{F}_{2},x)
vanishes for
x\in\{\mathrm{Q}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{1}^{1},\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{2}^{1}\}
,
where
\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{1}^{1}
and
\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{2}^{1}
are the elements in
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
dual to
\upxi_{1}
and
\upxi_{2}
, respectively.

Let
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{K})
. The
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-projective as it is a summand of

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\Sigma^{-5}\mathcal{K}^{\wedge 6})\cong\Sigma^{-5}\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}6},

which is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free. However,
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
is a graded local ring, and over a local ring, being projective is equivalent to being free. Hence,
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-free. Since
\mathrm{Q}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}},\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{1}^{1},
and
\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{2}^{1}
are primitive modulo
(\rho,\tau)
, and for
\mathrm{K}:=\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
i\in\{0,1,2\}
and
t\in\{1,2\}

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{Q}_{i}^{\mathbb{R}})=2=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{K},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{t}^{1}),

it follows from
Proposition 5.5
that

\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbb{F}_{2},x)\cong\mathcal{M}(\overline{e}(\mathrm{K}^{\otimes 6}),x)\cong\overline{e}(\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{K},x)^{\otimes 6})=0

for
x\in\{{\mathrm{Q}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{1}^{1},\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}},\overline{\mathrm{P}}_{2}^{1}\}
.
Thus,
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is free over
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
.
Proposition 5.5
also implies that the
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-rank of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
is 64, and therefore

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})
has rank 1 over
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
.
∎

5.2.
An
\mathbb{R}
-motivic lift of
\mathcal{B}(2)

Let
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}=[\mathrm{Sq}^{4},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}]
. Unlike the classical Steenrod algebra,
\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
does not agree with
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Instead, as in
[
26
]
*Example 13.7, these are related by the formula

\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}=[\mathrm{Sq}^{4},\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\mathbb{R}}]+\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{5}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}.

However, one can check that both
\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
square to zero, hence generate exterior algebras. We define (left)
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-modules

\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{R}}(2):=\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)\otimes_{\Lambda(\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})}\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}

and

(5.7)

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2):=\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)\otimes_{\Lambda(\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}})}\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}.

Let
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
denote
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2})
. It is easy to check that the left ideal generated by
\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
(likewise
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
) in
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
is isomorphic to
\Sigma^{7,3}\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
(likewise
\Sigma^{7,3}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
).
It follows that there is an exact sequence of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-modules

(5.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{7,3}\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{R}}
is either
\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
or
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
. The main purpose of this subsection is to show that:

Lemma 5.9
.

There exists an exact sequence of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-modules whose underlying
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module exact sequence is isomorphic to (
5.8
) with
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
.

Remark 5.10
.

In the case of
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
, the image of
\Sigma^{7,1}\mathcal{B}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)\longrightarrow\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
is a sub-
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-module, but not a sub-
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module. See
Remark 5.15
for more details.

Lemma 5.9
and
Remark 5.10
are direct consequences of the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module structure of
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
which can be deduced from the injection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{5,1}\mathrm{A}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\otimes_{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}6},}

where
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{R}})
. We do not want to entirely leave this calculation to the reader because, without a few tricks, this calculation is likely to require computer assistance as
e
has
144

-basis. We begin after setting the following notation.

Remark 5.12
.

The lowest degree NYD which is not annihilated by
\overline{e}
is

3
1
0
1
0
0

which lives in degree
(5,1)
. Of course, there are multiple NYD’s in bidegree
(5,1)
not annihilated by
\overline{e}
but their images are the same. Likewise, the NYD of the highest degree not annihilated by
\overline{e}
is

1
3
7
3
7
7

which lives in bidegree
(28,11).

are annihilated by
\overline{{\sf R}}
.

Since the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Steenrod algebra is cocommutative we get

\overline{{\sf R}}(\overline{{\sf C}}(\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(-)))=\overline{{\sf R}}(\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\overline{{\sf C}}(-)))=\mathrm{Sq}^{i}(\overline{{\sf R}}(\overline{{\sf C}}(-))).

This, along with the Cartan formula, allows us to calculate
a\cdot\iota
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
, fairly easily. For example,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\cdot\iota

Figure 5.13.
\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module generators of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
. Black dots correspond to generators of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
and orange dots to
\Sigma^{7,3}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
.

Remark 5.14
.

We record (see
Figure 5.13
), in the notation introduced in
Subsection 2.1
, that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)}=

\displaystyle\{(0,0),(1,0),(2,1),(3,1),(4,1),(4,2),(5,2),(6,2),(6,3),(7,3),(8,3),(8,4),

\displaystyle(9,4),(10,4),(10,5),(11,5),(12,5),(12,6),(13,6),(14,6),(15,7),(16,7)\}

and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}}=\{(i+7\epsilon,j+3\epsilon):(i,j)\in\mathcal{D}_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)}\text{ and }\epsilon\in\{0,1\}\}.

Proof of
Lemma 5.9
.

Recall that the image of
\Sigma^{7,3}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
in (
5.8
) is the (left)
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
-submodule of
\mathrm{A}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
generated by
\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
. We must check that this is
closed under the action of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2},\mathrm{Sq}^{4}
are in
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
, it remains to check that for all
i\geq 3
and
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)

\mathrm{Sq}^{2^{i}}\cdot(a\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota)=b\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota

for some
b\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
.
For degree reasons (see
Remark 5.14
), we only need to consider the case when
i=3
and
a\in\{1,\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\}
. We check

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{8}\cdot(\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}+\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{2})\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{8}\cdot(\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Sq}^{7}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}+\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{7})\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sq}^{8}\cdot(\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}+\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}+\tau\mathrm{Sq}^{5}\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{1})\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\tilde{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota

and thus the result holds.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

We notice that

\mathrm{Sq}^{8}\cdot(\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota)=\overline{{\sf R}}(\raisebox{-10.00002pt}{
\hbox to21.45pt{\vbox to21.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.1892pt\lower-2.09888pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.87 L 7.87 7.87 L 7.87 15.75 L 15.75 15.75 L 15.75 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

cannot be equal to
b\mathrm{Q}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}\cdot\iota
for any
b\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
. This is an easy but tedious calculation.
For the convenience of the reader, we note that an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-basis for the elements in degree
|\mathrm{Sq}^{8}|=(8,4)
of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)
is given by

\{\mathrm{Sq}^{6}\mathrm{Sq}^{2},\tau\mathrm{Sq}^{7}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\tau\mathrm{Sq}^{5}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{7},\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{6}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{5}\mathrm{Sq}^{2},\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\rho^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{5}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\}.

5.3.
The construction of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}

Recall the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
-module
\tilde{\mathrm{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
, as given in (
5.7
), and let

\mathrm{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{2}:=\tilde{\mathrm{B}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{2}/(\rho).

Proof of
Theorem 1.24
.

Since
\mathrm{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{2}
is cyclic as an
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
-module, it admits a May filtration, whose associated graded is isomorphic to

\mathrm{gr}(\mathrm{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{2})\cong\Lambda(\upxi_{1,0},\upxi_{1,1},\upxi_{1,2},\upxi_{2,0},\upxi_{2,1})

and whose
\mathrm{E}_{2}
-page of the corresponding May spectral sequence is isomorphic to

(5.16)

{{}^{\mathrm{May}}}\mathrm{E}_{1,\mathrm{B}^{\mathbb{C}}_{2}}^{*,*,*,*}\cong\frac{\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}[{\sf h}_{i,j}:i\geq 1,j\geq 0]}{({\sf h}_{1,0},{\sf h}_{1,1},{\sf h}_{1,2},{\sf h}_{2,0},{\sf h}_{2,1})}.

From this and
Remark 5.14
, one easily checks that the condition for
Theorem 2.9
is satisfied. Thus, there exists
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathbf{Sp}_{2,\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}})\cong\tilde{\mathrm{B}}_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

Since, as an
\mathcal{A}(2)
-module

\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Re{(\upbeta(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}))})\cong\Re_{*}(\upbeta_{*}(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*,*}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}})))\cong\mathcal{B}(2),

the underlying spectrum of
\upbeta(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbb{R}})
is indeed one of the spectra
\mathcal{Z}
considered in
[
5
]
, and therefore of type
2
.

Appendix A
The
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Adem relations

Voevodsky established the motivic version of the Adem relations
[
26
]
*Section 10. However, his formulas contain some typos, so for the convenience of the reader, we here present the Adem relations, in the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic case.

Proposition A.1
.

In the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Steenrod algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{R}}
, the product
\mathrm{Sq}^{a}\mathrm{Sq}^{b}
is equal to

(1)

(
a
and
b
both even)

\sum_{j=0}^{a/2}\tau^{j\ \mathrm{mod}2}\binom{b-1-j}{a-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}

(2)

(
a
odd and
b
even)

\sum_{j=0}^{(a-1)/2}\binom{b-1-j}{a-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}+\rho\binom{b-j}{a-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j-1}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}.

(3)

(
a
even and
b
odd)

\sum_{j=0}^{a/2}\binom{b-1-j}{a-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}+\rho\binom{b-1-j}{a+1-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j-1}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}.

(4)

(
a
and
b
both odd)

\sum_{j=0}^{(a-1)/2}\binom{b-1-j}{a-2j}\mathrm{Sq}^{a+b-j}\mathrm{Sq}^{j}

Remark A.2
.

Given that
\mathrm{Sq}^{a}=\mathrm{Sq}^{1}\mathrm{Sq}^{a-1}
if
a
is odd and also that
\mathrm{Sq}^{1}(\tau)=\rho
, cases (2) and (4) follow from (1) and (3), respectively. Note also that (1) is the classical formula, but with
\tau
thrown in whenever needed to balance the weights. In formula (2), the left term appears only when
j
is even, while the second appears only when
j
is odd. In formula (3), the second term appears only when
j
is odd.

Example A.3
.

Some examples of the
\mathbb{R}
-motivic Adem relation in low degrees are

\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}=\tau\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},\qquad\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}=\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{1},

and

\mathrm{Sq}^{2}\mathrm{Sq}^{3}=\mathrm{Sq}^{5}+\mathrm{Sq}^{4}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}+\rho\mathrm{Sq}^{3}\mathrm{Sq}^{1}.
